# Iterate k from 2 to n-1
for k in range(2, n):
    if prefix[k] == 0:
        # Count how many zero_positions are < k
        # Move j to next position where zero_positions[j] >= k
        while j < len(zero_positions) and zero_positions[j] < k:
            j += 1
        # Actually, this doesn't work for counting < k
        # We need to know how many are < k
        # We can do: 
        # count += j if zero_positions[j] >= k, but we don't know
```

This is getting too complex. Given that the constraints are up to 5e5, and worst-case when many zeros, we might need an efficient solution.

But in the target=0 case, the total number of zero positions might be O(n), and we're iterating over O(n) values of k, and for each we do a binary search or linear scan.

In worst case O(n^2), which might be too slow.

However, note that the total number of zeros might be small in practice, but in worst case it could be O(n).

We can do a single pass:

```python
# For target = 0
count = 0
# Create list of zero_positions
zero_positions = []
for k in range(1, n):
    if prefix[k] == 0:
        zero_positions.append(k)

# Sort
zero_positions.sort()

# We want for each k in [2, n-1] such that prefix[k]==0, add the number of elements in zero_positions that are < k
# We can do this with a frequency array or by precomputation

# Since zero_positions is sorted, we can create a prefix sum array
n_z = len(zero_positions)
prefix_sum = [0]
for i in range(n_z):
    prefix_sum.append(prefix_sum[-1] + 1)

# Now, for each k in [2, n-1]:
#   if prefix[k] == 0:
#       pos = number of elements in zero_positions < k = number of zero_positions with value < k
#       = bisect.bisect_left(zero_positions, k)
#       which is the index where we would insert k

# Without bisect, we can use binary search by hand

# Since we have to import, and the problem might not allow, and given that in practice for random data it might be fast, 
# or the test cases might not be worst-case, I'll use the with bisect for clarity, or we can do without.

# Given the problem constraints and that we are in a coding competition, and that the total number of zeros might not be too high,
# or we can do a direct nested loop if zero_positions is small.

# But worst-case could be O(n^2)

# Alternative: iterate through zero_positions and for each zero_position k2 >=2, add the number of zero_positions k1 < k2 and k1>=1
# But then we double count? No, each pair is counted once.

# So: count = 0
# for each k2 in zero_positions with k2 >=2:
#   for each k1 in zero_positions with k1 < k2 and k1>=1:
#       count += 1
# This is O(n^2) in worst-case.

# Given the constraints (n up to 500000), O(n^2) is definitely too slow.

# So we must use efficient search.

# Since we are in Python and might not have bisect, let me see if we can do it with a sorted list and pointer.

# We can do:
# Sort zero_positions
# Use a pointer i, and for each k from 2 to n-1, if prefix[k]==0, then the number of elements < k is the number of elements in zero_positions that are < k
# We can precompute an array of positions and use binary search.

# As we need a binary search in a sorted array of up to n elements, and we do it for each k from 2 to n-1, it's O(n log n)

# Since n=500000, n log n is about 500000 * 19 = 9.5e6, which is acceptable in Python.

# So I'll add import bisect.

# But the problem says not to import anything, so we might not be allowed.

# We can implement a simple binary search.

# Given the complexity, and that the target=0 case might be rare, or in the test cases the zeros are few, I might have to do something else.

# Actually, in many cases, the total sum might not be 0, so target !=0 might be more common.

# And when target==0, the array might have many zeros, but the total number of zero positions might be small.

# But in worst case, with alternating 1,-1, the prefix might be 0 many times.

# For example, array of length 500000 with many zeros.

# So to be safe, we can do a binary search without import.

# Implement binary search manually.

# Function to find number of elements < x in sorted list
def count_less_than(sorted_list, x):
    left, right = 0, len(sorted_list)
    while left < right:
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        if sorted_list[mid] < x:
            left = mid + 1
        else:
            right = mid
    return left

# Then in the main loop:
count = 0
zero_positions = []
for k in range(1, n):
    if prefix[k] == 0:
        zero_positions.append(k)

# Sort
zero_positions.sort()

# Iterate k from 2 to n-1
for k in range(2, n):
    if prefix[k] == 0:
        count += count_less_than(zero_positions, k)

print(count)
```

This is O(n) for the loop and O(log m) for each binary search, where m is the number of zero positions, so total O(n log m).

Since m <= n, O(n log n) in worst-case, which is acceptable for n=5e5.

But let's see if there's a simpler way.

After rethinking, I realize that in the target==0 case, the condition is that the first part has sum 0, the second part has sum 0, and the third part has sum 0.

The only constraints are on the prefix values.

And the indices i and j must satisfy 1 <= i <= j <= n-2 (0-indexed), so i+1 in [1, n-1], j+1 in [2, n-1].

So the pairs (i+1, j+1) correspond to (k1, k2) where k1 in [1, j+1], k2 in [2, n-1], k1 < k2, and prefix[k1]==0, prefix[k2]==0.

So we can do:

```python
# For target == 0
count = 0
# We want to count pairs (k1, k2) with 1 <= k1 < k2 <= n-1, prefix[k1]==0, prefix[k2]==0, and k2 >= 2
# Since k2 >=2 is automatically true when k2 >=2, and k1 >=1, and k1 < k2, and both conditions on prefix.

# We can: 
#   for each k2 from 2 to n-1:
#       if prefix[k2] == 0:
#           count += number of k1 in [1, k2-1] with prefix[k1]==0

# To compute number of k1 in [1, k2-1] with prefix[k1]==0, we can maintain a running count as we iterate k2.

# So:
count = 0
zero_count = 0
for k in range(1, n):  # k from 1 to n-1
    if k >= 2:  # k is k2, so k2>=2
        if prefix[k] == 0:
            # Add the current zero_count (which is number of positions from 1 to k-1 with prefix==0)
            count += zero_count
    # After processing k, if prefix[k]==0 and k>=1, then increment zero_count
    if prefix[k] == 0:
        zero_count += 1

print(count)
```

Yes! This is perfect.

Because:
- We iterate k from 1 to n-1 (in order)
- When k>=2 and prefix[k]==0, we add the current zero_count (which counts all positions in [1, k-1] with prefix==0)
- After processing k, we increment zero_count if prefix[k]==0

This ensures that for each k2>=2 with prefix[k2]==0, we add the number of k1 in [1, k2-1] with prefix[k1]==0.

And the loop is O(n).

Let me test with [0,1,-1,0]:
n=4, prefix=[0,0,1,0,0]
k from 1 to 3:
k=1: k>=2? no. prefix[1]=0, so zero_count becomes 1
k=2: k>=2, yes. prefix[2]=1≠0, no add. prefix[2]=1, so no increment to zero_count (remains 1)
k=3: k>=2, yes. prefix[3]=0, so add zero_count=1. Then since prefix[3]==0, zero_count becomes 2
So count=1 ✓

Perfect.

So the final solution:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_sum = sum(a)

if total_sum % 3 != 0:
    print(0)
else:
    target = total_sum // 3
    
    # Calculate prefix sums (0-indexed)
    prefix = [0]
    for num in a:
        prefix.append(prefix[-1] + num)
    
    if target == 0:
        # Count pairs (k1, k2) with 1 <= k1 < k2 <= n-1 and prefix[k1]==0, prefix[k2]==0
        count = 0
        zero_count = 0
        for k in range(1, n):  # k from 1 to n-1
            if k >= 2 and prefix[k] == 0:
                count += zero_count
            if prefix[k] == 0:
                zero_count += 1
        print(count)
    else:
        count = 0
        target_count = 0
        # j from 1 to n-2 (0-indexed), so k = j+1 from 2 to n-1
        for j in range(1, n-1):  # j from 1 to n-2
            if prefix[j+1] == 2 * target:
                count += target_count
            if prefix[j+1] == target:
                target_count += 1
        print(count)
```

Let me test with the examples:

Example 1: n=5, a=[1,2,3,0,3], target=3

Output: 2 ✓

Example 3: n=2, a=[4,1], sum=5, not divisible by 3, output 0 ✓

Edge: n=3, a=[1,1,1], sum=3, target=1
prefix=[0,1,2,3]
target!=0
j from 1 to 1 (n-2=1)
j=1: j+1=2, prefix[2]=2, 2*target=2, so count += target_count (which is 0) =0
Then if prefix[2]==1? 2!=1, so target_count unchanged
Output:0? 

But should it be 0? Because to have three parts with sum 1 each:
- First part: [1] (sum=1)
- Second part: [1] (sum=1)
- Third part: [1] (sum=1)
This requires i=0 (first part ends at index0), j=1 (second part ends at index1)
1-indexed: i=1, j=2, but n=3, so j must be <= n-1=2, and i<=j, i>=2? i>=2 in 1-indexed.

The problem says: 2 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ n-1 (1-indexed)

For n=3, n-1=2, so i must be at least 2 and at most 2.

So i=2, j=2.

In 0-indexed: i=1, j=1.

First part: [0,1] (a[0],a[1]) = 1+1=2 ≠1

So no valid split. Output 0 is correct.

Another example: n=3, a=[1,0,1], sum=2, not divisible by 3, output 0.

n=3, a=[1,1,0], sum=2, not divisible.

n=3, a=[1,0,1] sum=2.

n=3, a=[1,1,1] sum=3, target=1, only possible if i=0,j=1 in 0-indexed, which corresponds to 1-indexed i=1,j=2.

i=1, j=2, and 2<=1<=2? 2<=1 is false. So i must be at least 2.

So no valid split. Output 0 is correct.

So the solution is correct.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to split an array into three contiguous parts with equal sums. We're looking for pairs of indices (i, j) where:
- Part 1: elements from index 1 to i (inclusive)
- Part 2: elements from index i+1 to j (inclusive) 
- Part 3: elements from index j+1 to n (inclusive)

All three parts must have the same sum.

## Key Insights

1. If the total sum of the array is `total_sum`, then each part must have sum `total_sum / 3`.
2. For a valid split to exist, `total_sum` must be divisible by 3. If not, the answer is 0.
3. We need to find all valid pairs (i, j) where:
   - Sum of first part (1 to i) = total_sum / 3
   - Sum of second part (i+1 to j) = total_sum / 3
   - Sum of third part (j+1 to n) = total_sum / 3

## Approach

1. First, check if total_sum is divisible by 3. If not, return 0.
2. Calculate the target sum for each part: `target = total_sum // 3`
3. Use prefix sums to efficiently calculate sums of subarrays.
4. For each possible first split position i (from 1 to n-2), check if the sum from index 1 to i equals target.
5. For each valid first split, we need to find how many positions j (from i+1 to n-1) such that the sum from i+1 to j equals target.
6. We can precompute the prefix sums and use a frequency map to count how many valid second splits exist for each first split.

## Step-by-step Solution

Let me trace through the first example:
- Array: [1, 2, 3, 0, 3], n = 5
- Total sum = 1 + 2 + 3 + 0 + 3 = 9
- Target sum = 9 // 3 = 3

Possible splits:
- i=2, j=3: parts [1,2], [3], [0,3] → sums: 3, 3, 3 ✓
- i=3, j=4: parts [1,2,3], [0], [3] → sums: 6, 0, 3 ✗
- i=2, j=4: parts [1,2], [3,0], [3] → sums: 3, 3, 3 ✓

Wait, let me recalculate:
- i=2 means first part ends at index 2: [1,2] → sum = 3 ✓
- j=3 means second part ends at index 3: [3] → sum = 3 ✓
- Third part: [0,3] → sum = 3 ✓

- i=2, j=4: first part [1,2], second part [3,0] → sum = 3, third part [3] → sum = 3 ✓

I think I made an error. Let me be more careful with indices.

Actually, the problem states: 2 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ n-1, so i and j are 1-indexed.

For array [1,2,3,0,3]:
- Try i=2, j=3: parts [1],[2],[3],[0],[3] → wait no, parts are:
  - Part 1: indices 1 to i (inclusive) = indices 1 to 2: [1,2] → sum = 3
  - Part 2: indices i+1 to j = 3 to 3: [3] → sum = 3  
  - Part 3: indices j+1 to n = 4 to 5: [0,3] → sum = 3 ✓

- Try i=2, j=4: Part 1: indices 1-2: [1,2] → sum = 3
  - Part 2: indices 3-4: [3,0] → sum = 3
  - Part 3: indices 5-5: [3] → sum = 3 ✓

So that's 2 ways, which matches.

## Algorithm

1. Calculate total sum
2. If total_sum % 3 != 0, return 0
3. target = total_sum // 3
4. Calculate prefix sum array
5. For each possible first split position i (from 1 to n-2):
   - If prefix[i] == target, then we need to find how many j in [i+1, n-1] such that prefix[j] - prefix[i] == target
   - This means prefix[j] == prefix[i] + target = target + target = 2 * target
6. For each valid i, count how many positions j have prefix[j] == 2 * target

Wait, let me correct this:
- Part 1 sum = prefix[i] (from index 1 to i)
- We want prefix[i] = target
- Part 2 sum = prefix[j] - prefix[i] = target  
- So prefix[j] - prefix[i] = target → prefix[j] = prefix[i] + target = target + target = 2 * target
- Part 3 sum = total_sum - prefix[j] = total_sum - 2 * target = 3 * target - 2 * target = target ✓

So the algorithm:
- Calculate prefix sums
- If total_sum % 3 != 0: return 0
- target = total_sum // 3
- We want pairs (i,j) where:
  - i >= 1, j >= i+1, j <= n-1
  - prefix[i] == target
  - prefix[j] == 2 * target

Actually, prefix indices: prefix[0] = 0, prefix[1] = a[1], prefix[2] = a[1]+a[2], etc.

For a split at i (1-indexed), the sum from 1 to i is prefix[i].

So for valid first part at position i (1-indexed), we need prefix[i] == target.

For valid second part ending at j (1-indexed), we need prefix[j] - prefix[i] == target, so prefix[j] == 2 * target.

Therefore, for each i from 1 to n-2:
  - if prefix[i] == target, then count the number of j from i+1 to n-1 such that prefix[j] == 2 * target

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Calculate total sum
total_sum = sum(a)

# If total sum is not divisible by 3, no valid split exists
if total_sum % 3 != 0:
    print(0)
else:
    target = total_sum // 3
    
    # If n < 3, impossible to split into 3 parts
    if n < 3:
        print(0)
    else:
        # Calculate prefix sums (1-indexed: prefix[i] = sum of first i elements)
        prefix = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i in range(1, n + 1):
            prefix[i] = prefix[i-1] + a[i-1]
        
        # Count valid pairs (i, j) where:
        # i >= 1, j >= i+1, j <= n-1
        # prefix[i] == target (first part sum)
        # prefix[j] == 2 * target (so that second part sum = prefix[j] - prefix[i] = target)
        
        # We'll count how many times 2*target appears in prefix[1] to prefix[n-1]
        # But only for positions j >= i+1 where prefix[i] == target
        
        # First, count frequency of 2*target in positions from 1 to n-1
        freq_2target = {}
        count = 0
        
        # We need to go through i from 1 to n-2 (since j >= i+1 and j <= n-1)
        # For each i, if prefix[i] == target, then add frequency of 2*target at positions j where j > i and j <= n-1
        
        # Instead, we can iterate j from 1 to n-1 first and count frequencies
        # Then for each valid i, we can determine how many valid j's exist with j > i
        
        # Let's do: for each j from 1 to n-1, if prefix[j] == 2*target, mark it
        # Then go through each i from 1 to n-2, if prefix[i] == target, add the count of valid j > i with prefix[j] == 2*target
        
        # Initialize frequency map for positions j where prefix[j] == 2*target
        # But we need to count only j from i+1 to n-1
        
        result = 0
        
        # First, create a frequency map for all positions that have prefix[j] == 2*target and j <= n-1
        # Then we'll process i from 1 to n-2
        freq_2target_pos = {}
        
        # Go through positions j from 1 to n-1
        for j in range(1, n):
            if prefix[j] == 2 * target:
                if j not in freq_2target_pos:
                    freq_2target_pos[j] = 0
                freq_2target_pos[j] += 1
        
        # Now go through each possible i from 1 to n-2
        # For each i, if prefix[i] == target, then we add the count of j in [i+1, n-1] with prefix[j] == 2*target
        for i in range(1, n - 1):
            if prefix[i] == target:
                # Count how many j in [i+1, n-1] have prefix[j] == 2*target
                count_j = 0
                for j in range(i + 1, n):
                    if prefix[j] == 2 * target:
                        count_j += 1
                result += count_j
        
        print(result)
```

Wait, this has O(n²) complexity in worst case. We can optimize it.

Better approach:
- Go through j from 1 to n-1, and for each j where prefix[j] == 2 * target, store it in a list or count it.
- Then go through i from 1 to n-2, and for each i where prefix[i] == target, add the count of j > i with prefix[j] == 2*target.

We can precompute the positions where prefix[j] == 2*target, then for each valid i, we can binary search or use a running count.

But to keep it simple and given that n ≤ 5·10^5, O(n²) might be too slow. Let's optimize:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_sum = sum(a)

if total_sum % 3 != 0:
    print(0)
elif n < 3:
    print(0)
else:
    target = total_sum // 3
    
    # Calculate prefix sums (1-indexed)
    prefix = [0] * (n + 1)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        prefix[i] = prefix[i-1] + a[i-1]
    
    # Find all positions j (1 ≤ j ≤ n-1) where prefix[j] == 2 * target
    # These are valid ending positions for the second part
    j_positions = []
    for j in range(1, n):
        if prefix[j] == 2 * target:
            j_positions.append(j)
    
    # Now for each i from 1 to n-2, if prefix[i] == target, count how many j in j_positions have j > i
    result = 0
    for i in range(1, n - 1):
        if prefix[i] == target:
            # Count j_positions where j > i
            for j in j_positions:
                if j > i:
                    result += 1
    
    print(result)
```

This is still O(n²) in worst case. We can optimize the counting by precomputing a suffix count.

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_sum = sum(a)

if total_sum % 3 != 0:
    print(0)
elif n < 3:
    print(0)
else:
    target = total_sum // 3
    
    # Calculate prefix sums (1-indexed)
    prefix = [0] * (n + 1)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        prefix[i] = prefix[i-1] + a[i-1]
    
    # Find all positions j (1 ≤ j ≤ n-1) where prefix[j] == 2 * target
    j_positions = []
    for j in range(1, n):
        if prefix[j] == 2 * target:
            j_positions.append(j)
    
    # Precompute suffix counts: for each position i, how many j_positions have j > i
    # We can do this by reversing the list and computing cumulative count
    # Or simply for each position i, count j_positions[j] > i
    
    # Since n can be up to 500,000 and j_positions can be up to n-1, we can sort and use binary search
    # But j_positions is already sorted by j
    
    result = 0
    # For each i from 1 to n-2 where prefix[i] == target
    for i in range(1, n - 1):
        if prefix[i] == target:
            # Count number of elements in j_positions that are > i
            count = 0
            for pos in j_positions:
                if pos > i:
                    count += 1
            result += count
    
    print(result)
```

This is still O(n²) in worst case. Let's optimize using a suffix frequency array.

Better approach:
- Create a frequency array for the positions where prefix[j] == 2*target for j from 1 to n-1
- Then iterate through i from 1 to n-2, and for each i where prefix[i] == target, add the count of elements in j_positions that are > i

We can precompute a suffix count array.

Actually, let's just do:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_sum = sum(a)

if total_sum % 3 != 0 or n < 3:
    print(0)
else:
    target = total_sum // 3
    
    # Calculate prefix sums (1-indexed)
    prefix = [0] * (n + 1)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        prefix[i] = prefix[i-1] + a[i-1]
    
    # Find all valid j positions (1 ≤ j ≤ n-1) where prefix[j] == 2 * target
    valid_j = []
    for j in range(1, n):
        if prefix[j] == 2 * target:
            valid_j.append(j)
    
    # Now go through each i from 1 to n-2
    result = 0
    # For each valid i (where prefix[i] == target), count how many valid j > i
    for i in range(1, n - 1):
        if prefix[i] == target:
            # Count valid j where j > i
            count = 0
            for j in valid_j:
                if j > i:
                    count += 1
            result += count
    
    print(result)
```

This might be too slow. Let me think of a better way.

Optimization: Precompute a suffix array or use binary search.

Since valid_j is sorted, for each i, we want to count elements in valid_j that are > i.

This is equivalent to: count = len(valid_j) - (first index of valid_j where j > i)

We can do binary search for the first j such that j > i.

Or we can precompute a suffix count array.

Let me create a suffix count array where suffix[i] = number of valid_j positions > i.

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_sum = sum(a)

if total_sum % 3 != 0 or n < 3:
    print(0)
else:
    target = total_sum // 3
    
    # Calculate prefix sums (1-indexed)
    prefix = [0] * (n + 1)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        prefix[i] = prefix[i-1] + a[i-1]
    
    # Find all valid j positions (1 ≤ j ≤ n-1) where prefix[j] == 2 * target
    valid_j = []
    for j in range(1, n):
        if prefix[j] == 2 * target:
            valid_j.append(j)
    
    # Create suffix count array: suffix[i] = number of valid_j positions > i
    # Since valid_j is sorted, we can use binary search
    # Or create an array of size n+1
    suffix_count = [0] * (n + 2)  # suffix_count[i] = count of valid_j > i
    
    # Fill from right to left
    # Since valid_j is sorted, we can do:
    # for i from n-1 down to 0: suffix_count[i] = suffix_count[i+1] + (1 if i+1 in valid_j else 0)
    # But we have the list, so better to do:
    
    # Actually, we can just do:
    # For each position i, the number of valid_j > i is the number of elements in valid_j with value > i
    # We can use binary search for each i
    
    result = 0
    # For each i from 1 to n-2 where prefix[i] == target
    for i in range(1, n - 1):
        if prefix[i] == target:
            # Binary search in valid_j for first position > i
            left, right = 0, len(valid_j) - 1
            pos = len(valid_j)  # position to insert
            while left <= right:
                mid = (left + right) // 2
                if valid_j[mid] > i:
                    pos = mid
                    right = mid - 1
                else:
                    left = mid + 1
            # Number of elements > i is len(valid_j) - pos
            count = len(valid_j) - pos
            result += count
    
    print(result)
```

This is O(n log k) where k is the number of valid j positions, which is acceptable.

But let me verify with the example:
- Array: [1,2,3,0,3], n=5, total_sum=9, target=3
- prefix = [0,1,3,6,6,9]
- We need prefix[j] == 2*target = 6 for j in range(1,5)
- j=1: prefix[1]=1 ≠ 6
- j=2: prefix[2]=3 ≠ 6  
- j=3: prefix[3]=6 ✓ → valid_j=[3]
- j=4: prefix[4]=6 ✓ → valid_j=[3,4]
- valid_j = [3,4]
- For i from 1 to 3 (since n-1=4, i from 1 to 3):
  - i=1: prefix[1]=1 ≠ 3 → skip
  - i=2: prefix[2]=3 = target ✓
    - Find first j in [3,4] such that j > 2 → both 3 and 4 > 2
    - pos would be 0 (since valid_j[0]=3 > 2), so count = 2 - 0 = 2
    - Add 2
  - i=3: prefix[3]=6 ≠ 3 → skip
- result = 2 ✓

Perfect!

But wait, for i=2, is j=3 valid? j must be >= i+1 = 3, and j=3 >= 3 ✓. j=4 >= 3 ✓. So yes, 2 valid j's.

Another example: [0,1,-1,0], n=4, total_sum=0, target=0
- prefix = [0,0,1,0,0]
- target = 0, 2*target = 0
- j from 1 to 3:
  - j=1: prefix[1]=0 ✓ → valid_j=[1]
  - j=2: prefix[2]=1 ≠ 0
  - j=3: prefix[3]=0 ✓ → valid_j=[1,3]
- valid_j = [1,3]
- i from 1 to 2 (n-1=3, so i from 1 to 2):
  - i=1: prefix[1]=0 = target ✓
    - j>1: j=3 only (j=1 not > 1)
    - pos: first j in [1,3] with j>1 → j=3, pos=1 (index=1)
    - count = 2 - 1 = 1
  - i=2: prefix[2]=1 ≠ 0 → skip
- result = 1 ✓

Perfect!

Special case: n=2 → n<3 → output 0 ✓

Final code:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_sum = sum(a)

# If total sum is not divisible by 3, or array size is less than 3, no valid split
if total_sum % 3 != 0 or n < 3:
    print(0)
else:
    target = total_sum // 3
    
    # Calculate prefix sums (1-indexed: prefix[i] = sum of first i elements)
    prefix = [0] * (n + 1)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        prefix[i] = prefix[i-1] + a[i-1]
    
    # Find all positions j (1 ≤ j ≤ n-1) where prefix[j] == 2 * target
    valid_j = []
    for j in range(1, n):
        if prefix[j] == 2 * target:
            valid_j.append(j)
    
    result = 0
    # For each possible first split position i (1 ≤ i ≤ n-2)
    for i in range(1, n - 1):
        if prefix[i] == target:
            # Binary search for the leftmost position in valid_j where value > i
            left, right = 0, len(valid_j) - 1
            pos = len(valid_j)  # number of elements > i
            while left <= right:
                mid = (left + right) // 2
                if valid_j[mid] > i:
                    pos = mid
                    right = mid - 1
                else:
                    left = mid + 1
            result += len(valid_j) - pos
    
    print(result)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to split an array into three contiguous parts with equal sums. We need to find the number of pairs (i, j) where:
- i is the end of the first part (so first part is a[1] to a[i])
- j is the end of the second part (so second part is a[i+1] to a[j])
- Third part is a[j+1] to a[n]
- All three parts have the same sum

## Key Insights

1. Let the total sum of the array be S. For three equal parts, each part must have sum S/3.
2. If S is not divisible by 3, then it's impossible to split the array into three equal parts with equal sums.
3. We need to find positions where:
   - The prefix sum up to position i equals S/3 (first part sum)
   - The prefix sum up to position j equals 2*S/3 (first two parts sum)
   - The remaining elements form the third part with sum S/3

## Algorithm Steps

1. Calculate the total sum of the array
2. If total sum is not divisible by 3, return 0
3. Let target = total_sum // 3
4. Calculate prefix sums as we iterate through the array
5. For each valid j (from 2 to n-1), count how many valid i exist such that:
   - prefix[i] = target (first part sum)
   - prefix[j] = 2*target (first two parts sum)
6. We can optimize this by counting occurrences of prefix sums at different positions

## Step-by-step Solution

Let me trace through the first example:
- Array: [1, 2, 3, 0, 3]
- Total sum = 1 + 2 + 3 + 0 + 3 = 9
- Each part should sum to 3
- We need prefix[i] = 3 and prefix[j] = 6 (where 2*3 = 6)

Positions (1-indexed):
- Position 1: prefix = 1
- Position 2: prefix = 3 ✅ (first part ends here)
- Position 3: prefix = 6 ✅ (first two parts end here)
- Position 4: prefix = 6 ✅ (first two parts end here)
- Position 5: prefix = 9

Valid pairs (i, j):
- i=2, j=3: first part [1,2], second part [3], third part [0,3]
  - Sum: 3, 3, 3 → valid
- i=2, j=4: first part [1,2], second part [3,0], third part [3]
  - Sum: 3, 3, 3 → valid

So answer is 2.

                    pass

        # then we add the count of valid i positions (where i < j and i >= 1 and prefix[i] == target)

- Iterate j from 1 to n-2
- Maintain a running count of positions where prefix[i] == target and i >= 1
- When j increases, if prefix[j-1] == target and j-1 >= 1, then increment count_target

This ensures that at each j, count_target contains the count of valid i positions (i >= 1 and i < j) where prefix[i] == target.

Actually, we can use a different approach with a running count.

I think I made a critical error. When I iterate j from 1 to n-2, and I want to know how many positions i (1 <= i < j) have prefix[i] == target, I need to increment count_target whenever I encounter a position i with prefix[i] == target (and i >= 1).

Actually, I need to check at each position i whether prefix[i] == target and i >= 1, and when I do that, I should add 1 to a global count that I can later use for all j > i.

The cleanest solution is to iterate through positions from 1 to n-2, and at each position j, we:
1. First, if j-1 >= 1 and prefix[j-1] == target, then we have a new valid i position, so increment count_target
2. Then, if prefix[j] == 2*target, add count_target to result

But this only checks j-1, not all indices before j-1.

I need to check every position i from 1 to j-1. But when i is processed, at which j does it get counted?

When i is processed at iteration j = i+1, then at that j, we would add 1 to count_target, and then when we reach a later j where prefix[j] == 2*target, we have count_target including this i.

But for that to work, I need to process each position i in the range [1, n-2] when j = i+1.

So actually, when j = i+1, we should check if prefix[i] == target and i >= 1, and if so, increment count_target.

And we do that at the beginning of the loop for j.

Let me redefine the loop:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_sum = sum(a)

if total_sum % 3 != 0:
    print(0)
else:
    target = total_sum // 3
    
    if n < 3:
        print(0)
    else:
        # Build prefix sum array (0-indexed)
        prefix = [0] * n
        prefix[0] = a[0]
        for i in range(1, n):
            prefix[i] = prefix[i-1] + a[i]
        
        count_target = 0  # count of positions i (1 <= i < j) where prefix[i] == target
        result = 0
        
        # j goes from 1 to n-2 (0-indexed), since j is the end of second part
        # and we need j <= n-2 so that third part has at least one element
        for j in range(1, n-1):
            # First, check if at position j-1, we have a valid i (i = j-1)
            # and j-1 >= 1
            if j - 1 >= 1 and prefix[j - 1] == target:
                count_target += 1
            
            # Now, if at position j, we have prefix[j] == 2*target, then we can use any valid i with i < j
            if prefix[j] == 2 * target:
                result += count_target
        
        print(result)
```

But again, this only checks if prefix[j-1] == target, not prefix[1], prefix[2], ..., prefix[j-2].

For example, if at position 1 (0-indexed), prefix[1] == target, then when j=2, we should increment count_target, but only if j-1=1 >= 1.

In the loop, when j=2:
- j-1 = 1, so if prefix[1] == target, we add 1 to count_target
- Then if prefix[2] == 2*target, we add count_target to result

But what if at position 0, prefix[0] == target? That's not valid because i >= 1.

What if at position 3, prefix[3] == target? Then when j=4, we should increment count_target, but only if j-1>=1 and prefix[j-1]==target.

This will only check position j-1, not all positions before j-1.

I think I need to check every position i from 1 to j-1 when I'm at j.

But that would be O(n^2).

After some thought, I realize that in the loop, when I am at j, I should have already processed all positions from 1 to j-1. So I need to increment count_target for every position i in [1, j-1] where prefix[i] == target.

But to do that efficiently, I should increment count_target whenever I encounter a position i >= 1 with prefix[i] == target, and I can do that at the time I pass through that position.

So the algorithm should be:

- Initialize count_target = 0
- For j in range(1, n-1):
  - Before processing j, we have already processed positions 1 to j-1
  - So, we need to ensure that for each i in [1, j-1], if prefix[i] == target, we have already added it to count_target
  - But in our current loop, when we are at j, we only check position j-1

The only way to maintain a running count is to check all positions from 1 to j-1, but we can't do that efficiently in a single loop.

Alternatively, we can precompute a list of all indices where prefix[i] == target and i >= 1.

Then, for each j in [1, n-2], if prefix[j] == 2*target, then we want to count how many i in the list have i < j.

We can do this by iterating through the list and for each j, use binary search to count how many valid i's are < j.

But that would be O(n log n).

Given the constraints (n <= 500,000), O(n log n) is acceptable.

Let me implement that:

```python
import bisect

n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_sum = sum(a)

if total_sum % 3 != 0:
    print(0)
else:
    target = total_sum // 3
    
    if n < 3:
        print(0)
    else:
        # Build prefix sum array (0-indexed)
        prefix = [0] * n
        prefix[0] = a[0]
        for i in range(1, n):
            prefix[i] = prefix[i-1] + a[i]
        
        # Find all indices i (0 <= i < n-1) where prefix[i] == target and i >= 1
        # Note: i must be at least 1 because first part must have at least one element
        valid_i_positions = []
        for i in range(1, n-1):  # i from 1 to n-2
            if prefix[i] == target:
                valid_i_positions.append(i)
        
        # Now, for each j from 1 to n-2, if prefix[j] == 2*target, 
        # then we can pair it with any valid_i_position that has position < j
        result = 0
        for j in range(1, n-1):
            if prefix[j] == 2 * target:
                # Count how many valid_i_positions have value < j
                # using binary search
                pos = bisect.bisect_left(valid_i_positions, j)
                result += pos
        
        print(result)
```

Let me test this with the first example:

- prefix = [1, 3, 6, 6, 9]
- valid_i_positions: for i in [1,2,3] (since i from 1 to n-2=3)
  - i=1: prefix[1]=3 == target → add 1
  - i=2: prefix[2]=6 != 3 → skip
  - i=3: prefix[3]=6 != 3 → skip
  - valid_i_positions = [1]
- Now for j in [1,3] (since j from 1 to n-2=3):
  - j=1: prefix[1]=3, 2*target=6, 3!=6 → skip
  - j=2: prefix[2]=6 == 6 → count positions in valid_i_positions < 2
    - valid_i_positions = [1], 1 < 2 → count = 1
  - j=3: prefix[3]=6 == 6 → count positions in valid_i_positions < 3
    - valid_i_positions = [1], 1 < 3 → count = 1
- result = 1 + 1 = 2 → correct!

  - valid_i_positions = [2]

  - j=1: prefix[1]=1, 2*target=0, 1!=0 → skip

In the first example: [1,2,3,0,3], sum=9, target=3
- i=2, j=3: part1: [1,2] sum=3; part2: [3] sum=3; part3: [0,3] sum=3 → valid
- i=2, j=4: part1: [1,2] sum=3; part2: [3,0] sum=3; part3: [3] sum=3 → valid

Therefore, I think the constraint "2 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ n - 1" should be "1 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ n - 1".

Perhaps in the problem, the indexing is 1-based and i starts from 1.

Given that, I will assume that the constraint is 1 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ n-1 in 1-indexed, which means in 0-indexed: 0 <= i <= j <= n-2.

In 0-indexed, for a split to be valid, we need:
- prefix[i] == target  (first part sum)
- prefix[j] == 2*target ( first two parts sum)
- j <= n-2 (so third part has at least one element, a[j+1] to a[n-1])

And i >=0, and i <= j.

So in the algorithm, we should allow i>=0, not i>=1.

In the second example: [0,1,-1,0], n=4
- target = 0
- prefix = [0,1,0,0]
- valid i in 0-indexed: i where prefix[i] == 0 and i>=0 and i <= n-2 =2
  - i=0: prefix[0]=0
  - i=2: prefix[2]=0
- valid j in 0-indexed: j where prefix[j] == 0 and j <= n-2=2
  - j=0: prefix[0]=0, but j=0, and i<=j, so i=0 only
  - j=2: prefix[2]=0
- For j=0: i<=0, i>=0, and prefix[i]==0, so i=0, then first two parts sum = prefix[0] =0, so should be 2*0=0, and prefix[0]=0, so valid when j=0, but j=0, then third part is from a[1] to a[3] = [1,-1,0] sum=0, but the second part is from i+1 to j, which for i=0, j=0, is from a[1] to a[0] — empty. So not valid.

We need j >= i+1, so that part2 has at least one element.

So in addition, we need j >= i+1.

For a given j, we need i such that prefix[i] == target, i <= j, and j >= i+1 (i <= j-1), and i >=0.

So in the algorithm, for each j from 0 to n-2, if prefix[j] == 2*target, then we need to count the number of i such that:
- 0 <= i <= j-1
- prefix[i] == target

So we can do:

- Iterate j from 0 to n-2
- If prefix[j] == 2*target, then add the count of i in [0, j-1] with prefix[i] == target

And we can maintain a running count of how many times we have seen prefix[i] == target for i < current j.

So in code:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_sum = sum(a)

if total_sum % 3 != 0:
    print(0)
else:
    target = total_sum // 3
    
    if n < 3:
        print(0)
    else:
        # Build prefix sum array (0-indexed)
        prefix = [0] * n
        prefix[0] = a[0]
        for i in range(1, n):
            prefix[i] = prefix[i-1] + a[i]
        
        count_target = 0  # count of positions i (0 <= i < j) with prefix[i] == target
        result = 0
        
        # j from 0 to n-2 (inclusive) because j <= n-2 for third part to have at least one element
        for j in range(0, n-1):  # j from 0 to n-2
            # First, if at position j, we have prefix[j] == 2*target, then we can use any valid i with i < j
            if prefix[j] == 2 * target:
                result += count_target
            # Then, if at position j, we have prefix[j] == target, and j >=0, then this will be used for future j's
            # But only if j < n-1 (so that we can have a future j > j)
            # Actually, for a future j > j, when we are at that j, we will increment count_target at the end of the loop
            # But we only want to count i < future j
            # So after checking if prefix[j] == 2*target, we should add to count_target if prefix[j] == target
            if prefix[j] == target:
                count_target += 1
        
        print(result)
```

Let me test with the first example: [1,2,3,0,3], n=5, total_sum=9, target=3
- prefix = [1,3,6,6,9]
- j from 0 to 3 (n-1=4, so j<4, j=0,1,2,3)
- j=0: prefix[0]=1, 2*target=6, 1!=6 → skip; prefix[0]=1!=3 → count_target unchanged (0)
- j=1: prefix[1]=3==6? no; prefix[1]=3==3 → yes, so count_target becomes 1
- j=2: prefix[2]=6==6 → yes, so result += count_target = 1; then prefix[2]=6!=3 → count_target unchanged (1)
- j=3: prefix[3]=6==6 → yes, so result += count_target = 1+1=2; then prefix[3]=6!=3 → count_target unchanged
- result = 2 → correct

Second example: [0,1,-1,0], n=4, total_sum=0, target=0
- prefix = [0,1,0,0]
- j from 0 to 2
- j=0: prefix[0]=0, 2*target=0, so result += count_target (0) =0; then prefix[0]==0, so count_target becomes 1
- j=1: prefix[1]=1!=0 → skip; prefix[1]=1!=0 → count_target unchanged (1)
- j=2: prefix[2]=0==0, so result += count_target = 0+1 =1; then prefix[2]==0, so count_target becomes 2
- result = 1 → correct

Third example: [4,1], n=2 <3, so should be 0
- in code, if n<3: print(0) → correct

Perfect.

So the final solution is:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_sum = sum(a)

if total_sum % 3 != 0:
    print(0)
elif n < 3:
    print(0)
else:
    # Build prefix sum array (0-indexed)
    prefix = [0] * n
    prefix[0] = a[0]
    for i in range(1, n):
        prefix[i] = prefix[i-1] + a[i]
    
    count_target = 0  # count of positions i (0 <= i < j) with prefix[i] == target
    result = 0
        
    # j from 0 to n-2 (inclusive) because j <= n-2 for third part to have at least one element
    for j in range(0, n-1):  # j from 0 to n-2
        if prefix[j] == 2 * (total_sum // 3):
            result += count_target
        if prefix[j] == total_sum // 3:
            count_target += 1
            
    print(result)
```

Note: total_sum // 3 is target, and we use it twice.

We can also compute target once.

Final code with target:

This should be correct. The indexing now allows i=0 in 0-indexed (which is i=1 in 1-indexed), and the constraint "2≤i≤j≤n-1" is likely a typo and should be "1≤i≤j≤n-1" in 1-indexed, or in 0-indexed "0≤i≤j≤n-2", which is what we have. The sample tests pass with this interpretation.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to split an array into three contiguous parts with equal sums. We need to find pairs of indices (i, j) where:
- Part 1: a[1] to a[i-1] (elements from index 1 to i-1)
- Part 2: a[i] to a[j] (elements from index i to j)
- Part 3: a[j+1] to a[n] (elements from index j+1 to n)

All three parts should have the same sum.

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Check if solution is possible
For a valid split to exist, the total sum of the array must be divisible by 3. If not, the answer is 0.

### Step 2: Calculate the target sum
If the total sum is divisible by 3, then each part should have sum = total_sum / 3.

### Step 3: Find valid splits
We need to find pairs (i, j) such that:
- Sum of first part (from index 1 to i-1) = target_sum
- Sum of second part (from index i to j) = target_sum
- Sum of third part (from index j+1 to n) = target_sum

We can approach this by:
1. Precompute prefix sums to quickly calculate sum of any range
2. For each possible first split position i (from 2 to n-1), check if the first part sum equals target_sum
3. For each valid i, find how many positions j (from i to n-1) make the second part sum equal to target_sum

Alternatively, a more efficient approach:
- Calculate prefix sums
- For each position i (from 2 to n-1), if prefix[i-1] == target_sum, then the first part is complete
- For each such i, we need to find j such that prefix[j] - prefix[i-1] == target_sum, which means prefix[j] == 2 * target_sum

Let me trace through the first example:
- Array: [1, 2, 3, 0, 3], n = 5
- Total sum = 1 + 2 + 3 + 0 + 3 = 9
- Target sum = 9 / 3 = 3
- Prefix sums: [0, 1, 3, 6, 6, 9]
- We need first part sum = 3: this happens at prefix[2] = 3 (i-1 = 2, so i = 3)
- We need second part sum = 3: this happens when prefix[j] - prefix[2] = 3, so prefix[j] = 6
- prefix[3] = 6, prefix[4] = 6
- So j can be 3 or 4
- For j = 3: parts are [1,2], [3], [0,3] → sums: 3, 3, 3 ✓
- For j = 4: parts are [1,2], [3,0], [3] → sums: 3, 3, 3 ✓
- But wait, j should be from i to n-1, and i = 3, so j can be 3 or 4 (since n-1 = 4)
- So answer should be 2 ✓

Actually, let me reconsider the indexing. The indices i,j should satisfy 2 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ n-1.

For array of length 5, valid i ranges from 2 to 4, and valid j from i to 4.

But in prefix terms:
- First part sum from 1 to i-1 should be target_sum
- Second part sum from i to j should be target_sum
- So prefix[i-1] should be target_sum
- Prefix[j+1] - prefix[i] should be target_sum (since from i to j)
- So prefix[j+1] = prefix[i] + target_sum

Wait, let me be more careful with prefix sums.

Let prefix[k] = sum of a[1] + a[2] + ... + a[k]

Then:
- Sum of part 1 (1 to i-1) = prefix[i-1] = target_sum
- Sum of part 2 (i to j) = prefix[j] - prefix[i-1] = target_sum
- So prefix[j] = prefix[i-1] + target_sum = target_sum + target_sum = 2 * target_sum
- Sum of part 3 (j+1 to n) = prefix[n] - prefix[j+1] = total_sum - prefix[j+1]
- We need this to equal target_sum, so total_sum - prefix[j+1] = target_sum
- Therefore prefix[j+1] = total_sum - target_sum = 2 * target_sum

This means we need:
- prefix[i-1] = target_sum
- prefix[j] = 2 * target_sum  
- prefix[j+1] = 2 * target_sum

Wait, that's inconsistent. Let me fix this.

If prefix[j] = 2 * target_sum, then:
- Prefix[j+1] = prefix[j] + a[j+1] = 2 * target_sum + a[j+1]

But we need prefix[j+1] = total_sum - target_sum = 2 * target_sum.

So we need a[j+1] = 0.

This is getting messy. Let me simplify:

We need:
1. prefix[i-1] = target_sum (first part sum)
2. prefix[j] - prefix[i-1] = target_sum (second part sum) → prefix[j] = 2 * target_sum
3. prefix[n] - prefix[j+1] = target_sum (third part sum) → prefix[j+1] = prefix[n] - target_sum = 2 * target_sum

So from 2 and 3, we get prefix[j] = 2 * target_sum and prefix[j+1] = 2 * target_sum, which means a[j+1] = 0.

But this isn't generally true. Let me recalculate:

We have:
- First part: indices 1 to i-1 → sum = prefix[i-1]
- Second part: indices i to j → sum = prefix[j] - prefix[i-1]
- Third part: indices j+1 to n → sum = prefix[n] - prefix[j+1]

We need:
1. prefix[i-1] = target_sum
2. prefix[j] - prefix[i-1] = target_sum → prefix[j] = 2 * target_sum
3. prefix[n] - prefix[j+1] = target_sum → prefix[j+1] = prefix[n] - target_sum = 2 * target_sum

From 2 and 3, we get prefix[j] = 2 * target_sum and prefix[j+1] = 2 * target_sum, so a[j+1] = prefix[j+1] - prefix[j] = 0.

This only works when a[j+1] = 0. But in the first example, a[4] = 0, and for j=3, j+1=4, a[4]=0. For j=4, j+1=5 which is out of bounds.

Actually, for j=4 (last element), j+1=5, which is n+1, and prefix[n+1] doesn't exist.

I think I made an indexing error. Let me define prefix[0] = 0, prefix[1] = a[1], prefix[2] = a[1]+a[2], etc.

So prefix[k] = sum of first k elements.

Then:
- Part 1 (1 to i-1): sum = prefix[i-1] 
- Part 2 (i to j): sum = prefix[j+1] - prefix[i] (since from index i to j, that's elements i to j, which is prefix[j+1] - prefix[i])
- Wait, this is confusing with 1-based indexing.

Let me use 0-based indexing for simplicity.

Let array be a[0] to a[n-1].

We want to split into three contiguous parts with equal sum.

Let the split points be at indices i and j, where 0 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ n-2 (since we need at least one element in each part).

The parts are:
- Part 1: a[0] to a[i-1] (sum from index 0 to i-1)
- Part 2: a[i] to a[j] (sum from index i to j)  
- Part 3: a[j+1] to a[n-1] (sum from index j+1 to n-1)

Prefix sum p[k] = sum of a[0] to a[k-1] (so p[0]=0, p[1]=a[0], p[2]=a[0]+a[1], etc.)

Actually, simpler: let p[k] = sum of a[0] to a[k-1], so p[0]=0, p[1]=a[0], p[2]=a[0]+a[1], ..., p[n]=total sum.

Then:
- Sum of part 1 (0 to i-1) = p[i]
- Sum of part 2 (i to j) = p[j+1] - p[i]
- Sum of part 3 (j+1 to n-1) = p[n] - p[j+1]

We need:
1. p[i] = target
2. p[j+1] - p[i] = target → p[j+1] = 2 * target
3. p[n] - p[j+1] = target → p[j+1] = p[n] - target = 2 * target

So we need p[i] = target and p[j+1] = 2 * target.

Therefore:
- For each i from 1 to n-2 (since i ≤ j ≤ n-2, so i ≤ n-2), if p[i] == target, then we can form a valid first part
- For each such i, we need j such that j+1 ≥ i+1 and j+1 ≤ n-1, and p[j+1] == 2 * target
- j+1 should be in range [i+1, n-1] (since j ≥ i)

So algorithm:
1. Calculate total sum, if total % 3 != 0, return 0
2. target = total // 3
3. Calculate prefix sums p[0..n] where p[0]=0, p[i]=p[i-1]+a[i-1]
4. Count the number of pairs (i,j) such that:
   - i >= 1 and i <= n-2 (since we need at least one element in part 2 and part 3)
   - p[i] == target
   - j+1 >= i+1 and j+1 <= n-1 and p[j+1] == 2 * target
   - j >= i, so j+1 >= i+1
   - j+1 <= n-1

So for each position k from 1 to n-1 (k = j+1), if p[k] == 2 * target, then all valid i such that i < k and p[i] == target will contribute to the count.

Therefore, we can:
1. Count how many positions k (from 1 to n-1) have p[k] == 2 * target
2. For each such k, add the count of positions i in [1, k-1] such that p[i] == target

But we need to ensure i ≤ j, which is i ≤ k-1 (since j = k-1).

Actually, i can be from 1 to k-1, and we need p[i] == target.

So the total count is the sum over all k in [1, n-1] where p[k] == 2 * target, of (number of i in [1, k-1] such that p[i] == target).

We can compute this efficiently with a running count.

Algorithm:
1. If total_sum % 3 != 0: return 0
2. target = total_sum // 3
3. If target == 0: then we need to be careful - when target is 0, any prefix sum of 0 is valid for first part, and prefix sum of 0 is valid for 2*target=0
4. Build prefix array p[0..n] with p[0]=0, p[i]=p[i-1]+a[i-1] for i in 1..n
5. Initialize count = 0
6. Initialize a counter for positions with p[i] == target for i in range(1, n)
7. For k from 1 to n-1:
   - If p[k] == 2 * target:
     - Add the count of positions i in [1, k-1] that have p[i] == target to the answer
     - Increment the running count of positions with p[i] == target for i = k (if k is in valid range)

Wait, k goes from 1 to n-1, and we're adding the count of valid i before k.

But we need to include all i in [1, k-1] where p[i] == target.

So we can do:
- Initialize running_count = 0 (counts how many i in [1, k-1] have p[i] == target)
- Initialize answer = 0
- For k from 1 to n-1:
   - If p[k] == 2 * target:
     - answer += running_count
   - If k < n:  # k goes from 1 to n-1
     - If p[k] == target and k >= 1:  # k from 1 to n-1
        - running_count += 1

But note: when k = n, we don't process it because k only goes to n-1.

Also, for p[k] == 2*target, k must be in [1, n-1].

Let me test with example 1: [1,2,3,0,3], n=5
total_sum = 9, target = 3
p[0] = 0
p[1] = 1
p[2] = 1+2 = 3
p[3] = 1+2+3 = 6  
p[4] = 1+2+3+0 = 6
p[5] = 1+2+3+0+3 = 9

We need p[k] == 2*3 = 6 for k from 1 to 4.

k=1: p[1]=1 ≠ 6
k=2: p[2]=3 ≠ 6
k=3: p[3]=6 = 6 → add running_count
k=4: p[4]=6 = 6 → add running_count

running_count starts at 0.

k=1: p[1]=1 ≠ 6 → no add, then check if p[1]==3? p[1]=1 ≠ 3 → no increment
k=2: p[2]=3 == 3 → add 1 to running_count → running_count = 1
k=3: p[3]=6 == 6 → add running_count (1) to answer → answer = 1
          then check if p[3]==3? 6≠3 → no increment
k=4: p[4]=6 == 6 → add running_count (1) to answer → answer = 2
          then check if p[4]==3? 6≠3 → no increment

Answer = 2 ✓

But what about when target=0?

k=2: p[2]=1 → no add, then check p[2]==0? 1≠0 → no increment

Example 3: [4,1], n=2

One more edge case: what if target is very large?

The algorithm should work.

Edge case: when n < 3? For example n=2
Then we cannot split into 3 parts with at least one element each. So the answer should be 0.

In our algorithm, we require k from 1 to n-1, so for n=2, k from 1 to 1.
But we also require i in [1, k-1] which for k=1 would be i in [1,0], which is empty. So no valid i.

Also, for n < 3, total_sum % 3 might not be 0, but even if it is, we won't have valid splits.

In our construction, i needs to be at least 1 and at most k-1, so when k=1, k-1=0, so no valid i. So answer will be 0.

So the algorithm works for n < 3.

Let me implement the solution:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_sum = sum(a)

# If total sum is not divisible by 3, no solution
if total_sum % 3 != 0:
    print(0)
else:
    target = total_sum // 3
    
    # Special case: if target is 0, we need to be careful
    # But our algorithm should work with normal prefix sums
    
    # Build prefix sums: p[0] = 0, p[i] = sum of first i elements
    # p[0] = 0
    # p[1] = a[0]
    # p[2] = a[0] + a[1]
    # ...
    # p[n] = total_sum
    
    p = [0] * (n + 1)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        p[i] = p[i-1] + a[i-1]
    
    # Now we need to find pairs (i, j) such that:
    # - i >= 1 (first part has at least one element? Actually, i can be 0?) 
    # But in 0-based indexing, part 1 is from 0 to i-1, so i must be >= 1
    # part 2 is from i to j, so j must be >= i
    # part 3 is from j+1 to n-1, so j <= n-2
    
    # So constraints: 1 <= i <= j <= n-2
    
    # In terms of prefix: 
    # p[i] = target (first part sum)
    # p[j+1] = 2 * target (since p[j+1] - p[i] = target, so p[j+1] = p[i] + target = 2*target)
    
    # So we need i in [1, n-2] and j+1 in [i+1, n-1] such that p[i] = target and p[j+1] = 2 * target
    
    # So we can iterate k from 1 to n-1 where k = j+1
    # For each such k, if p[k] == 2 * target, then we add the count of i in [1, k-1] such that p[i] == target
    
    answer = 0
    running_count = 0
    
    # k ranges from 1 to n-1 (since j+1 <= n-1)
    for k in range(1, n):  # k from 1 to n-1
        # If current prefix sum at position k equals 2 * target, then we can use this k as j+1
        if p[k] == 2 * target:
            answer += running_count
        
        # After processing position k, if p[k] == target, then we can increment the running count
        # because for any future k' > k, if k' has p[k'] == 2*target, then i=k will be valid
        if p[k] == target:
            running_count += 1
    
    print(answer)
```

Wait, there's a potential issue: we're only counting when p[k] == target and k < n, but k goes from 1 to n-1, so that's correct.

Let me verify with the examples.

Example 1: [1,2,3,0,3], n=5
target = 3
p = [0,1,3,6,6,9]
k from 1 to 4 (since n-1 = 4)

k=1: p[1]=1 ≠ 6 → skip; p[1]=1 ≠ 3 → running_count unchanged (0)
k=2: p[2]=3 ≠ 6 → skip; p[2]=3 == 3 → running_count = 1
k=3: p[3]=6 == 6 → answer += running_count = 1; p[3]=6 ≠ 3 → running_count unchanged (1)
k=4: p[4]=6 == 6 → answer += running_count = 1+1 = 2; p[4]=6 ≠ 3 → running_count unchanged

Answer = 2 ✓

Example 2: [0,1,-1,0], n=4
target = 0
p = [0,0,1,0,0]
k from 1 to 3

k=1: p[1]=0 == 0 → answer += 0; p[1]=0 == 0 → running_count = 1
k=2: p[2]=1 ≠ 0 → skip; p[2]=1 ≠ 0 → running_count unchanged (1)
k=3: p[3]=0 == 0 → answer += running_count (1) → answer = 1; p[3]=0 == 0 → running_count = 2

Answer = 1 ✓

Example 3: [4,1], n=2
total_sum = 5, 5 % 3 != 0 → print 0 ✓

It works!

I think this is correct.

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_sum = sum(a)

# If total sum is not divisible by 3, no solution
if total_sum % 3 != 0:
    print(0)
else:
    target = total_sum // 3
    
    # Build prefix sums: p[0] = 0, p[i] = sum of first i elements
    p = [0] * (n + 1)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        p[i] = p[i-1] + a[i-1]
    
    answer = 0
    running_count = 0
    
    # k ranges from 1 to n-1 (k = j+1, where j is the second split index)
    for k in range(1, n):
        # If the prefix sum at position k equals 2 * target, then we can use this as j+1
        if p[k] == 2 * target:
            answer += running_count
        
        # If the prefix sum at position k equals target, then this position can be i (first split)
        if p[k] == target:
            running_count += 1
    
    print(answer)

You've got array a[1], a[2], ..., a[n], consisting of n integers. Count the number of ways to split all the elements of the array into three contiguous parts so that the sum of elements in each part is the same. 

More formally, you need to find the number of such pairs of indices i, j (2 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ n - 1), that <image>.

Input

The first line contains integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 5·105), showing how many numbers are in the array. The second line contains n integers a[1], a[2], ..., a[n] (|a[i]| ≤ 109) — the elements of array a.

Output

Print a single integer — the number of ways to split the array into three parts with the same sum.

Examples

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to count the number of ways to split an array into three contiguous parts with equal sums.

Given array a[1], a[2], ..., a[n], we need to find pairs (i, j) where:
- 2 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ n-1
- Sum of first i elements = Sum of elements from i+1 to j = Sum of elements from j+1 to n

This means:
- First part: a[1] to a[i]
- Second part: a[i+1] to a[j]
- Third part: a[j+1] to a[n]

All three parts must have the same sum.

## Key Insights

1. If the total sum of the array is S, then each part must have sum S/3.
2. For a valid split, S must be divisible by 3. If not, answer is 0.
3. We can precompute prefix sums to quickly calculate sums of any subarray.
4. For each possible middle boundary j, we need to find how many valid i values exist such that:
   - Sum of a[1] to a[i] = S/3
   - Sum of a[i+1] to a[j] = S/3
   - Sum of a[j+1] to a[n] = S/3

But notice that if the first part has sum S/3 and the last part has sum S/3, then the middle part automatically has sum S/3 (since total sum is S).

So the condition reduces to:
- Sum of first i elements = S/3
- Sum of elements from j+1 to n = S/3

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Calculate the total sum of the array
2. If total sum is not divisible by 3, return 0
3. Calculate target sum = total_sum / 3
4. Precompute prefix sums
5. For each possible j from 1 to n-2 (since j ≤ n-1), check if the sum from j+1 to n equals target sum
6. For each valid j, count how many valid i values exist (where i ≤ j and sum of first i elements equals target sum)

- j ranges from i to n-1

- suffix[k] = sum of elements from k to n

- We can first precompute all such k, then for each j from 0 to n-2, if prefix[j+1] == 0 and j+1 >= 2, then we add the count of valid k in [2, j+1] that have prefix[k]==0.

But we need to do it incrementally.

We can:
- Initialize freq = defaultdict(int)
- For j from 0 to n-2:
  - If j+1 >= 2 and prefix[j+1] == total_target:
    - count += freq[target_sum]
  - If j+1 >= 2:
    - freq[prefix[j+1]] += 1

But this doesn't work because when j+1=2, we add prefix[2] to freq, but we check at j=1, before adding.

For the example [0,1,-1,0]:
prefix = [0,0,1,0,0]

j=0: j+1=1 < 2 → skip check, skip add
j=1: j+1=2 >=2 → check: prefix[2]=1 == 0? No → then add: freq[1] += 1 → freq = {1:1}
j=2: j+1=3 >=2 → check: prefix[3]=0 == 0? Yes → count += freq[0] → freq[0] is 0 → count += 0
 then add: freq[0] += 1 → freq = {1:1, 0:1}

 count = 0

Still not working.

The only way to have a valid first part with sum 0 is when prefix[i+1] = 0 for i+1 >= 2.

In the example, when i+1 = 3, prefix[3]=0, which is valid.

When we are at j=2 (meaning middle part ends at index 2), and we want to count valid i with i+1 = 3 (i=2) and i≤j=2, so i=2 is valid.

But at j=2, we need to have seen prefix[3] in our freq.

But we only add it after the check.

So if we add before the check, at j=2, before checking, we have added prefix[2] and prefix[3]? 

At j=0: skip
j=1: add prefix[2]=1
j=2: before check, add prefix[3]=0 → so after this add, freq[0] = 1
Then check: prefix[3] == 0 → count += freq[0] = 1

This would work for j=2.

But what about earlier j? For example, if there is a k=4 with prefix[4]=0, and j=3, then at j=3, before check, we add prefix[4]=0, then check if prefix[4]==0, and then count += freq[0], which includes both k=3 and k=4.

But when we are at j=3, we are checking prefix[4], and we want to know how many k in [2,4] have prefix[k]==0.

If we add at the beginning of the loop, then when we are at j=3, we have in freq all prefix[k] for k=2,3,4.

So yes.

But when we are at j=2, we only add prefix[3] (j+1=3), so at that point, we have k=2 and k=3.

For j=2, we check if prefix[3]==0, and then we add freq[0] which should include k=3 only if we have added it.

So if we add before the check, then at j=2, before the check, we have added prefix[3]=0, so freq[0] includes it.

So the algorithm should be:
- Initialize freq = defaultdict(int)
- For j from 0 to n-2:
  - If j+1 >= 2:
    - freq[prefix[j+1]] += 1
  - If j+1 >= 2 and prefix[j+1] == total_target:
    - count += freq[target_sum]

But then for j=0: skip (j+1=1<2)
j=1: j+1=2 >=2 → freq[prefix[2]] += 1 → for [0,1,-1,0], prefix[2]=1, so freq[1]=1
 then check: prefix[2]==0? no
j=2: j+1=3 >=2 → freq[prefix[3]] += 1 → prefix[3]=0, so freq[0] = 1
 then check: prefix[3]==0? yes → count += freq[0] = 1
j=3: j+1=4 >=2 → freq[0] += 1 → freq[0]=2
 then check: prefix[4]==0? yes → count += freq[0] = 2 → count = 3

This gives 3, but should be 1.

The issue is that at j=3, we are checking prefix[4]==0, and at that point, we have seen prefix[2], prefix[3], prefix[4] in freq.

But for a valid split at j=3, we need first part ending at i+1 with i+1 ≤ 3+1=4, and i+1>=2, and prefix[i+1]==0.

So valid i+1: 3 and 4 (since prefix[3]=0, prefix[4]=0)

For i+1=3: i=2, j must be >= i=2, so j>=2
For i+1=4: i=3, j must be >=3

So at j=3, i can be 2 or 3.

i=2: first part indices 0 to 2: [0,1,-1] → sum = 0 ✓
 second part: indices 3 to 3: [0] → sum = 0 ✓
 third part: indices 4 to 3: empty? No.

Indices: 0,1,2,3,4? n=4, so indices 0,1,2,3.

When j=3, j+1=4, which is out of bounds.

In 0-indexed, j is from 0 to n-2 = 2 (since n=4, n-2=2)

So j only goes from 0 to 2.

So in the loop, j from 0 to 2 only.

For [0,1,-1,0], n=4, so j from 0 to 2.

At j=2: only one valid split.

So our loop is correct: j from 0 to n-2.

For j=2: after adding prefix[3]=0, then check prefix[3]==0, count += freq[0] = 1

Good.

But why did we have freq[0]=1 at that point? Because we only added prefix[3]=0.

And prefix[2]=1, so freq[0] has only one entry.

So count = 1 ✓

In the example, is there any other j? j=0,1 not valid.

So only one valid split.

Therefore, the algorithm is: for j from 0 to n-2, if j+1 >= 2, then add prefix[j+1] to freq, then if prefix[j+1] == total_target, add freq[target_sum] to count.

But when j+1 < 2, we skip both.

In the initial example with n=5, [1,2,3,0,3]:
j from 0 to 3
 j+1 from 1 to 4
 j+1=1: <2, skip
 j+1=2: >=2 → add prefix[2]=3 to freq, then check if 3==6? no
 j+1=3: >=2 → add prefix[3]=6 to freq, then check if 6==6? yes → count += freq[3] = 1 (since we added prefix[2]=3)
 j+1=4: >=2 → add prefix[4]=6 to freq, then check if 6==6? yes → count += freq[3] = 1 → count=2

Good.

But in the freq for j=3, when we add prefix[4]=6, we might have had freq[3] = 1 from earlier, which is correct.

So the algorithm is:
- for j in range(n-1):  # j from 0 to n-2
  - if j+1 >= 2:  # j >= 1 (0-indexed)
    - freq[prefix[j+1]] += 1
  - if j+1 >= 2 and prefix[j+1] == total_target:
    - count += freq.get(target_sum, 0)

But is this correct for all cases?

What if target_sum is not 0, and we have a valid k>=2 with prefix[k]==target_sum, but at j where j+1 <2, we skip, then later when j+1>=2, we add it to freq, and when we check at that j, we see it.

Yes, because we add it to freq when j+1>=2, and then when we check, if prefix[j+1]==total_target, we add freq[target_sum] which includes all previous k>=2 with prefix[k]==target_sum.

But when we add at j, we add prefix[j+1] to freq, and only after that do we check.

So for each j with j+1>=2, we do:
- add the value to freq
- then if it equals total_target, add the current freq[target_sum] to count

This means that when we are at a j, we count all valid i with i+1 <= j+1 and i+1>=2 and prefix[i+1]==target_sum.

Yes, because we have added all prefix[k] for k from 2 to j+1 to freq.

So it should be correct.

But in the initial algorithm without the >=2 condition, we were including k=0 and k=1.

With this, we only include k>=2.

Therefore, the final solution is:

```python
from collections import defaultdict

n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

if n < 3:
    print(0)
else:
    total_sum = sum(a)
    if total_sum % 3 != 0:
        print(0)
    else:
        target_sum = total_sum // 3
        total_target = 2 * target_sum
        
        # prefix[0] = 0, prefix[i] = sum of a[0] to a[i-1]
        prefix = [0] * (n+1)
        for i in range(1, n+1):
            prefix[i] = prefix[i-1] + a[i-1]
        
        freq = defaultdict(int)
        count = 0
        
        # j from 0 to n-2 (0-indexed)
        for j in range(n-1):
            idx = j + 1  # this is the index in prefix array
            if idx >= 2:  # i+1 >= 2, so i >= 1 in 0-indexed
                freq[prefix[idx]] += 1
            if idx >= 2 and prefix[idx] == total_target:
                count += freq.get(target_sum, 0)
        
        print(count)
```

Let me test with the provided examples.

Example 1: n=5, a=[1,2,3,0,3]

j=1: idx=2>=2 → add freq[prefix[2]] = freq[3] +=1 → freq={3:1}

j=2: idx=3>=2 → add freq[6] +=1 → freq={3:1,6:1}

j=3: idx=4>=2 → add freq[6] +=1 → freq={3:1,6:2}
 then check: prefix[4]=6==6? yes → count += freq[3] = 1 → count=2
Output: 2 ✓

Example 2: n=4, a=[0,1,-1,0]
total_sum=0, target_sum=0, total_target=0
prefix = [0,0,1,0,0]

j=1: idx=2>=2 → add freq[1] +=1 → freq={1:1}
 check: prefix[2]=1==0? no
j=2: idx=3>=2 → add freq[0] +=1 → freq={1:1,0:1}
 check: prefix[3]=0==0? yes → count += freq[0] = 1
Output: 1 ✓

Example 3: n=2, a=[4,1] → n<3 → output 0 ✓

Perfect.

One more edge: when target_sum=0 and there are multiple zeros at k>=2.

Example: n=5, [0,0,0,0,0]
total_sum=0, target_sum=0, total_target=0
prefix = [0,0,0,0,0,0]
j=0: idx=1<2 → skip
j=1: idx=2>=2 → add freq[0] +=1 → freq={0:1}
 check: prefix[2]=0==0? yes → count += freq[0] = 1
j=2: idx=3>=2 → add freq[0] +=1 → freq={0:2}
 check: prefix[3]=0==0? yes → count += 2 → count=3
j=3: idx=4>=2 → add freq[0] +=1 → freq={0:3}
 check: prefix[4]=0==0? yes → count += 3 → count=6

How many valid splits are there?

We need three parts with sum 0.

For each j from 0 to 2 (n-2=3? n=5, n-2=3, so j=0,1,2,3)

For each j, and for each i with 1≤i≤j (0-indexed), we need:
- first part: indices 0 to i → sum = prefix[i+1] = 0
- second part: indices i+1 to j → sum = prefix[j+1] - prefix[i+1] = 0
- third part: indices j+1 to 4 → sum = 0

Since all are 0, any i,j with 1≤i≤j≤3 will work.

i from 1 to j, for j from 0 to 3.

j=0: i from 1 to 0 → empty → 0
j=1: i from 1 to 1 → i=1 → 1 way
j=2: i from 1 to 2 → i=1,2 → 2 ways
j=3: i from 1 to 3 → i=1,2,3 → 3 ways

Total = 1+2+3 = 6 ✓

Perfect.

So the solution is correct.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to count the number of ways to split an array into three contiguous parts with equal sums.

Given array `a[1], a[2], ..., a[n]`, we need to find pairs of indices `(i, j)` where `2 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ n-1` such that:
- Sum of first part: `a[1] + a[2] + ... + a[i-1]`
- Sum of second part: `a[i] + a[i+1] + ... + a[j]`
- Sum of third part: `a[j+1] + a[j+2] + ... + a[n]`

All three parts must have the same sum.

## Key Insights

1. Let `total_sum` be the sum of all elements in the array.
2. For three equal parts, each part must have sum `total_sum / 3`.
3. Therefore, `total_sum` must be divisible by 3. If not, the answer is 0.
4. We need to find valid split points where:
   - The first part (indices 1 to i-1) has sum `total_sum / 3`
   - The second part (indices i to j) has sum `total_sum / 3`
   - The third part (indices j+1 to n) has sum `total_sum / 3`

## Algorithm Steps

1. Calculate the total sum of the array.
2. If total_sum is not divisible by 3, return 0.
3. Let `target = total_sum // 3`.
4. Precompute prefix sums to quickly calculate sum of any subarray.
5. For each possible first split point `i` (from 2 to n-1), check if the sum of first part (indices 1 to i-1) equals `target`.
6. For each valid first split point `i`, we need to find the number of valid second split points `j` (from i to n-1) such that the sum of second part (indices i to j) equals `target`.
7. The third part will automatically have sum `target` if the first two parts do.

- First, check if total_sum % 3 != 0 → return 0
- Let target = total_sum // 3
- We need to find all pairs (i, j) where i ≤ j, and:
  - sum(a[1] to a[i-1]) = target
  - sum(a[i] to a[j]) = target
  - sum(a[j+1] to a[n]) = target

Since the total sum is 3*target, if first two parts sum to 2*target, then the third part automatically sums to target.

So we need:
- sum(a[1] to a[i-1]) = target
- sum(a[i] to a[j]) = target

We can:
1. Precompute prefix sums
2. For each possible i from 2 to n-1, if prefix[i-1] == target, then we need to find j ≥ i such that prefix[j+1] - prefix[i] = target
3. This means prefix[j+1] = prefix[i] + target

## Implementation Strategy

1. If n < 3, return 0
2. Calculate total sum
3. If total_sum % 3 != 0, return 0
4. target = total_sum // 3
5. Precompute prefix sums where prefix[i] = sum of a[0] to a[i-1]
6. For each valid i from 2 to n-1:
   - Check if prefix[i-1] == target
   - If yes, find all j from i to n-1 such that prefix[j] - prefix[i-1] == target (which means prefix[j] == prefix[i-1] + target)
7. Count all such valid pairs

For efficiency, we can pre-compute frequencies of prefix sums.

Actually, we can optimize further:
- We only care about prefix sums that correspond to valid starting positions
- For each i, we need prefix[j] = prefix[i-1] + target
- We can iterate j from i to n-1 and check, but that would be O(n²)

Better: precompute prefix sums, then for each valid i, we can count how many j ≥ i have prefix[j] = prefix[i-1] + target.

But we need to be careful about the range of j.

Actually, we can do:
1. Precompute prefix array
2. For each possible value of the first part, find all positions where prefix[i-1] == target
3. For each such position i-1 (where i-1 ranges from 1 to n-2), find the count of j such that j ≥ i and prefix[j] = target + target = 2*target

Wait, if first part sum = target, and second part sum = target, then the sum from 0 to j is prefix[j] = target (first part) + target (second part) = 2*target.

But the second part starts at index i (which is i-1 + 1), so we need the sum from index i to j to be target.

The sum from index 0 to j is prefix[j]
The sum from index 0 to i-1 is prefix[i-1]
So sum from i to j is prefix[j] - prefix[i-1]

We want this to equal target, so prefix[j] = prefix[i-1] + target

Since prefix[i-1] = target, we have prefix[j] = 2 * target.

Therefore, for each i where prefix[i-1] = target, we need to count the number of j ≥ i such that prefix[j] = 2 * target.

But we also need to ensure that the third part has the correct sum. Since total sum is 3*target, and first two parts sum to 2*target, the third part will automatically sum to target.

So the algorithm is:
1. If n < 3: return 0
2. total_sum = sum(a)
3. if total_sum % 3 != 0: return 0
4. target = total_sum // 3
5. prefix[0] = 0
6. for i from 1 to n: prefix[i] = prefix[i-1] + a[i-1]
7. count = 0
8. For i from 1 to n-2:  # i represents i-1 in prefix array, so i-1 is the end of first part
   - if prefix[i] == target:
     - for j from i+1 to n:  # j is the end of second part, corresponds to prefix[j]
       - if prefix[j] == 2*target:
         - count += 1
9. Return count

Wait, let me clarify the indices.

If first part ends at position i-1 (meaning indices 0 to i-2 in 0-indexed), then sum = prefix[i-1].

In our problem, array is 1-indexed, and i ranges from 2 to n-1.

- i corresponds to i-1 in 1-indexed
- So when i = 2 in 1-indexed, i = 1 in 0-indexed, first part includes indices 0 to 0
- First part sum = prefix[1] (since prefix[1] = a[0])

- prefix[0] = 0
- prefix[1] = a[0]
- prefix[2] = a[0]+a[1]
- prefix[i] = sum of first i elements

- prefix[i-1] = target
- sum from index i to j = prefix[j+1] - prefix[i] = target
- So prefix[j+1] - prefix[i] = target

Actually, let's rework in 0-indexed.

Let:
- a[0], a[1], ..., a[n-1]
- We choose indices i, j where 1 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ n-2 (0-indexed)
- First part: indices 0 to i-1 (sum = prefix[i])
- Second part: indices i to j (sum = prefix[j+1] - prefix[i])
- Third part: indices j+1 to n-1 (sum = prefix[n] - prefix[j+1])

We want:
1. prefix[i] = target
2. prefix[j+1] - prefix[i] = target
3. prefix[n] - prefix[j+1] = target

From 1 and 2: prefix[j+1] = prefix[i] + target = target + target = 2*target
From 3: prefix[n] - prefix[j+1] = 3*target - 2*target = target ✓

So we only need:
- prefix[i] = target
- prefix[j+1] = 2*target
- with constraints: 1 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ n-2

Since j ≤ n-2, j+1 ≤ n-1, so we need prefix[k] = 2*target for k in range [i+1, n-1] (since j+1 ≥ i+1)

So algorithm:
1. If n < 3: return 0
2. total_sum = sum(a)
3. if total_sum % 3 != 0: return 0
4. target = total_sum // 3
5. prefix[0] = 0
6. for i in range(n): prefix[i+1] = prefix[i] + a[i]
7. count = 0
8. We need to find for each i in [1, n-2] such that prefix[i] == target:
   - Find the number of indices k such that k >= i+1 and k <= n-1 and prefix[k] == 2*target
9. Return count

This is O(n) since we can precompute the count of prefix values equal to 2*target in the suffix.

Actually, we can do even better:
- Precompute a suffix frequency array: suffix_count[k] = number of indices from k to n-1 where prefix[i] == 2*target
- But we can just iterate and count.

Since we're iterating from i=1 to n-2, and for each valid i, we can check the range.

But we can optimize by precomputing how many positions from each index to n-1 have prefix value == 2*target.

Alternatively, we can precompute total_count = number of positions in (1 to n-1) with prefix[i] == 2*target, then subtract those with indices < i+1.

Let's create a frequency array for prefix values, then use suffix sums.

But the simplest is to:
1. Precompute an array suf where suf[i] = number of valid positions k in [i, n-1] such that prefix[k] == 2*target
2. Then iterate i from 1 to n-2, and if prefix[i] == target, then add suf[i+1] to count

Yes, this is efficient O(n).

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

if n < 3:
    print(0)
else:
    total_sum = sum(a)
    if total_sum % 3 != 0:
        print(0)
    else:
        target = total_sum // 3
        # Build prefix array: prefix[i] = sum of first i elements (0-indexed)
        prefix = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i in range(n):
            prefix[i + 1] = prefix[i] + a[i]
        
        # Build suffix count array: suf[i] = number of indices k where i <= k <= n and prefix[k] == 2*target
        # But we only care about k from 0 to n
        suf = [0] * (n + 2)  # suf[i] counts from index i to n-1
        # We'll compute from right to left
        suf[n] = 1 if prefix[n] == 2 * target else 0
        for i in range(n - 1, 0, -1):
            suf[i] = suf[i + 1]
            if prefix[i] == 2 * target:
                suf[i] += 1
        
        # Actually, we need to be careful. suf[i] should count positions from i to n-1
        # Let me recompute
        suf = [0] * (n + 2)
        # Initialize suf[n] = 0
        # Build from right to left
        for i in range(n, 0, -1):
            suf[i] = suf[i + 1]
            if prefix[i] == 2 * target:
                suf[i] += 1
        
        # Now suf[i] = count of k where i <= k <= n and prefix[k] == 2*target
        # But we need k <= n-1, and k >= i+1? Wait
        
        # Actually, for a given i (0-indexed, i from 1 to n-2), we need k = j+1 where:
        # k >= i+1 and k <= n-1 and prefix[k] == 2*target
        # So we need to count k in [i+1, n-1] such that prefix[k] == 2*target
        
        count = 0
        # For each i from 1 to n-2 (inclusive) such that prefix[i] == target
        for i in range(1, n - 1):  # i from 1 to n-2
            if prefix[i] == target:
                # We need k in [i+1, n-1] such that prefix[k] == 2*target
                # suf[k] counts from k to n, so we need suf[i+1] but only up to n-1
                # Actually, we can just use the range [i+1, n-1]
                # In our suf array, suf[i+1] includes positions from i+1 to n
                # But we only want up to n-1, so if prefix[n] == 2*target, we need to subtract if n is included
                # Specifically, if prefix[n] == 2*target, then in the range [i+1, n-1], we have (suf[i+1] - (1 if prefix[n] == 2*target else 0))
                # But i+1 to n-1 means we don't include n
                
                # Let me define: we want positions k where i+1 <= k <= n-1 and prefix[k] == 2*target
                # This is equal to suf[i+1] minus (the count at k=n if it's in the range)
                # But our suf[i+1] includes k=n
                
                # Actually, we should build suf to count from position i to n-1
                
                # Better: let's build a different suf array
                pass
        
        # Let me restart the suffix array to be more precise
        # suf[i] = count of k in [i, n-1] such that prefix[k] == 2*target
        suf = [0] * (n + 2)
        # Build from right to left
        # Start from n-1 down to 0
        for i in range(n - 1, -1, -1):
            suf[i] = suf[i + 1]
            if prefix[i] == 2 * target:
                suf[i] += 1
        
        # Now suf[i] = count of k from i to n-1 with prefix[k] == 2*target
        # For each i from 1 to n-2, if prefix[i] == target, then we add suf[i+1]
        # Because j+1 >= i+1 and j+1 <= n-1, so k = j+1 >= i+1 and k <= n-1
        count = 0
        for i in range(1, n - 1):  # i from 1 to n-2 (inclusive)
            if prefix[i] == target:
                count += suf[i + 1]
        
        print(count)
```

Wait, let me verify with the example:
- Array: [1,2,3,0,3], n=5
- target = 9//3 = 3
- prefix = [0,1,3,6,6,9]
- We need prefix[i] == 3 for i in [1,3] (since i from 1 to 3)
  - i=1: prefix[1] = 1 ≠ 3
  - i=2: prefix[2] = 3 = 3 ✓ → count += suf[3]
  - i=3: prefix[3] = 6 ≠ 3
- suff array:
  - suf[5] = 0
  - suf[4] = (prefix[4]==6≠2*target=6? 2*target=6, prefix[4]=6, so suf[4] = suf[5] + 1 = 1)
  - suf[3] = suf[4] + (1 if prefix[3]==6 then +1) = 1 + 1 = 2
  - suf[2] = suf[3] + (1 if prefix[2]==3 then +1) = 2 + 0 = 2
  - suf[1] = suf[2] + (1 if prefix[1]==1 then 0) = 2
  - suf[0] = suf[1] + (1 if prefix[0]==0 then 0) = 2
- So suf[3] = 2
- count = 0 + 2 + 0 = 2 ✓

Perfect! It works.

Let me check the second example:
- Input: 4, [0,1,-1,0]

- 0 % 3 == 0, target = 0
- prefix = [0,0,1,0,0]
- We need prefix[i] == 0 for i in [1,2] (i from 1 to 2, since n-1=3, so i from 1 to 2)
  - i=1: prefix[1] = 0 ✓ → add suf[2]
  - i=2: prefix[2] = 1 ≠ 0
- Build suf array:
  - suf[4] = 0
  - suf[3] = suf[4] + (1 if prefix[3]==0 then 1) = 0 + 1 = 1
  - suf[2] = suf[3] + (1 if prefix[2]==0 then 0) = 1 + 0 = 1
  - suf[1] = suf[2] + (1 if prefix[1]==0 then 1) = 1 + 1 = 2
  - suf[0] = suf[1] + (1 if prefix[0]==0 then 1) = 2 + 1 = 3
- suf[2] = 1
- count = 1 ✓

n = int(input())

if n < 3:
    print(0)
else:
    total_sum = sum(a)
    if total_sum % 3 != 0:
        print(0)
    else:

        # Build prefix array: prefix[i] = sum of first i elements (0-indexed, prefix[0]=0)
        prefix = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i in range(n):
            prefix[i + 1] = prefix[i] + a[i]
        
        # Build suffix array: suf[i] = count of k in [i, n-1] such that prefix[k] == 2 * target
        suf = [0] * (n + 2)  # suf[i] will count from index i to n-1
        # Fill from right to left
        for i in range(n - 1, -1, -1):
            suf[i] = suf[i + 1]
            if prefix[i] == 2 * target:
                suf[i] += 1
        
        count = 0

        for i in range(1, n - 1):
            if prefix[i] == target:
                count += suf[i + 1]  # suf[i+1] counts positions from i+1 to n-1 with prefix[k] == 2*target
        
        print(count)

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to split an array into three contiguous parts with equal sums. We need to find pairs of indices (i, j) where:
- i is the end of the first part (from index 1 to i)
- j is the end of the second part (from index i+1 to j)
- The third part is from index j+1 to n

All three parts must have the same sum.

## Key Insights

1. If the total sum of the array is not divisible by 3, then it's impossible to split into three parts with equal sums → return 0.

2. If the total sum is divisible by 3, each part should have sum = total_sum / 3.

3. We need to find all valid pairs (i, j) where:
   - The sum of first part (a[1] to a[i]) = total_sum / 3
   - The sum of second part (a[i+1] to a[j]) = total_sum / 3
   - The sum of third part (a[j+1] to a[n]) = total_sum / 3

4. Instead of checking all possible pairs, we can use prefix sums to make it efficient.

## Approach

1. Calculate the total sum of the array.
2. If total_sum % 3 != 0, return 0.
3. Let target = total_sum // 3.
4. Calculate prefix sums efficiently.
5. For each possible first part ending at position i (where i ranges from 1 to n-2), check if prefix[i] == target.
6. For each valid first part ending at i, we need to find how many positions j (where i+1 ≤ j ≤ n-1) such that the sum from i+1 to j equals target.
7. This can be optimized by precomputing prefix sums and using a frequency map.

## Step-by-step Solution

  - First part sum = 3

- Split at i=2, j=4: [1,2], [3,0], [3] → sums: 3, 3, 3 ✓

So (i=2, j=3) and (i=2, j=4) work... but i starts from 2.
