## Algorithm

1. Calculate total sum
2. If total_sum % 3 != 0, return 0
3. target = total_sum // 3
4. Calculate prefix sum array
5. For each valid first part ending at position i (1 to n-2), check if prefix[i] == target
6. For each such i, we need to count how many j (i to n-2) such that the sum from i+1 to j equals target
7. The sum from i+1 to j = prefix[j] - prefix[i]
8. We want prefix[j] - prefix[i] = target, so prefix[j] = prefix[i] + target

Actually, let me refine:
- First part sum from 0 to i: prefix[i+1] (if we're 0-based)
- Second part sum from i+1 to j: prefix[j+1] - prefix[i+1]
- We want this to be target

Better approach:
- Let's work in 0-based indexing
- Total sum S
- Target = S // 3
- For each possible end of first part i (0 ≤ i ≤ n-3): first part is [0, i], sum = prefix[i+1]
- For each possible end of second part j (i+1 ≤ j ≤ n-2): second part is [i+1, j], sum = prefix[j+1] - prefix[i+1]
- We want: prefix[i+1] == target AND (prefix[j+1] - prefix[i+1]) == target
- Which means: prefix[i+1] == target AND prefix[j+1] == 2*target

So:
1. If total_sum % 3 != 0: return 0
2. target = total_sum // 3
3. Precompute prefix sums: prefix[0] = 0, prefix[i] = a[0] + a[1] + ... + a[i-1]
4. For each position k from 1 to n-1, if prefix[k] == target, it means a valid end for first part at k-1
5. For each such k, we want to count how many positions l (k+1 ≤ l ≤ n-1) such that prefix[l] == 2*target
6. But we also need to ensure the third part has sum target

Wait, actually:
- If prefix[i] == target, then sum of first part (indices 0 to i-1) is target
- If prefix[j] == 2*target, then sum of first two parts (indices 0 to j-1) is 2*target
- Then sum of third part (indices j to n-1) is total_sum - 2*target = target

So we need pairs (i,j) where:
- i is such that prefix[i] == target (first part ends at i-1)
- j is such that prefix[j] == 2*target (first two parts end at j-1)
- And i < j (since parts are contiguous)

So the algorithm:
1. total_sum = sum(a)
2. if total_sum % 3 != 0: return 0
3. target = total_sum // 3
4. prefix[0] = 0
5. for i in range(n): prefix[i+1] = prefix[i] + a[i]
6. Count how many positions k (1 to n-1) have prefix[k] == target
7. Count how many positions l (k+1 to n) have prefix[l] == 2*target
8. But we need to count pairs (k, l) where k < l, prefix[k] == target, prefix[l] == 2*target

Actually, we can do:
- For each position k from 1 to n-1 (inclusive), if prefix[k] == target, then we need to count how many positions l from k+1 to n such that prefix[l] == 2*target

But this would be O(n²) in worst case.

Better: Precompute frequency of prefix sums, then for each k from 1 to n-1 where prefix[k] == target, add count of prefix[l] == 2*target for l > k.

We can do this by iterating backwards.

Alternatively: Create a frequency map for prefix sums from position n down to 1.

Final algorithm:
1. total_sum = sum(arr)
2. if total_sum % 3 != 0: return 0
3. target = total_sum // 3
4. prefix = [0]
5. for i in range(n): prefix.append(prefix[-1] + arr[i])
6. count = 0
7. freq = {}  # frequency of prefix sums from position n down to 1
8. Start from the end: for i from n-1 down to 1:
9.   if prefix[i] == target: then we add freq.get(2*target, 0) to count
10.   then add prefix[i] to freq
11. return count

We want pairs (k, l) where k < l, prefix[k] == target, prefix[l] == 2*target.

But we need l > i, so we process from right to left.

Algorithm:
- Initialize count = 0
- Initialize a frequency dictionary freq for prefix sums (initialized empty)
- For i from n-1 down to 1:

Actually, when we process position i, we're looking at prefix[i], and we want to count how many positions j > i have prefix[j] == 2*target.

- Start from i = n-1 down to 1
- For each i:

2. if total_sum % 3 != 0: return 0
3. target = total_sum // 3
4. prefix = [0]

6. count = 0
7. freq = {}

9.   # at this i, the positions in prefix that are from i+2 to n are already in freq from previous steps, because we are going down
10.   # but we add at each step the value at position i+1 ( in prefix) for the next i
11.   # we add after we do the count

13.   if i+1 <= n-1: # i+1 in index of prefix, which is from 1 to n
14.     if prefix[i+1] == target:
15.        count += freq.get(2*target, 0)
16.   # then add prefix[i+1] to freq for future use ( for smaller i, when i' = i-1, then at i' we will have in freq values from (i-1)+2 = i+1 to n, and we will add prefix[i+1] when we go to i'=i-1)
17.   freq[prefix[i+1]] = freq.get(prefix[i+1], 0) + 1
18. return count

 in the example [0,1,-1,0]:
- n=4, target=0

- i=3: i+1=4, prefix[4]=0 ==0, so count += freq.get(0,0) = 0, then add prefix[4]=0 to freq → freq{0:1}
- i=2: i+1=3, prefix[3]=0 ==0, so count += freq.get(0,0) = 1, then add prefix[3]=0 to freq → freq{0:2}
- i=1: i+1=2, prefix[2]=1 !=0, count unchanged, then add prefix[2]=1 to freq → freq{0:2, 1:1}
- count = 1 ✓

 and for the split: at i=2 (0-based), which corresponds to i_1-based = i+1 = 3, and we have one l with l+1=3 ( because at i=2, we add after, and at that time, in freq, we have only from position i+2=4 to n=4, so only position 4, and at that time, when we do the count, we have at i=2, in freq only from 4 to 4, and we have 0 at position 4, so when we at i=2, we add count += freq.get(0,0) = 1, and that corresponds to l+1=4, so l=3 (0-based), which is valid for a second part ending at 3-1=2, and first part ending at i=2 (0-based), with i_1-based = 3, and j_1-based = l+1-1 = 3, so j=3, and j from i to n-1 in 1-based, and i=3, j=3, and j=3 <= n-1=3, so valid.

 and there is only one such l for i=2.

 so it works.

 in the first example: [1,2,3,0,3] n=5, target=3
- prefix = [0,1,3,6,6,9]
- i from 4 down to 1:
- i=4: i+1=5, prefix[5]=9 !=3, count +=0, then add prefix[5]=9 to freq → freq{9:1}
- i=3: i+1=4, prefix[4]=6 !=3, count +=0, then add prefix[4]=6 to freq → freq{9:1,6:1}
- i=2: i+1=3, prefix[3]=6 !=3, count +=0, then add prefix[3]=6 to freq → freq{9:1,6:2}
- i=1: i+1=2, prefix[2]=3 ==3, so count += freq.get(6,0) = 2, then add prefix[2]=3 to freq → freq{9:1,6:2,3:1}
- count = 2 ✓

 and it matches.

 in the third example: [4,1] n=2, total_sum=5, 5%3!=0, return 0 ✓

 so the algorithm is:

 total_sum = sum(arr)
 if total_sum % 3 != 0:
   return 0
 target = total_sum // 3
 prefix = [0]
 for i in range(n):
   prefix.append(prefix[-1] + arr[i])
 count = 0
 freq = {}
 for i in range(n-1, 0, -1):  # i from n-1 down to 1 (0-based)
   if prefix[i+1] == target:  # first part sum = prefix[i+1] - prefix[0] = prefix[i+1] = target
     count += freq.get(2*target, 0)
   freq[prefix[i+1]] = freq.get(prefix[i+1], 0) + 1
 return count

 in the loop, we go from i=n-1 down to 1, and at each i, we have in freq the values of prefix at positions from i+2 to n ( because when we have added for j from i+1 to n-1, with values at j+1 = from i+2 to n)

 and we only count when prefix[i+1] == target.

 and we add prefix[i+1] to freq after the count.

 this ensures that for a given i, we only have values from positions > i+1 in the frequency, which corresponds to l > i+1 in the second part end index, but in our indexing, l+1 is the position in prefix, and l+1 >= i+2, so l >= i+1, which is required.

 and each pair (i, l) with i < l is counted once, when at i, and l is in the frequency.

 so it should be correct. 

 let me test with the second example in a simple way: [0,1,-1,0] with n=4, we got count=1, correct.

 so I will implement this. 

 Note: i+1 in the loop, when i=n-1, i+1 = n, which is within prefix[0] to prefix[n] (index n).

 and when i=0, we don't enter because range(n-1,0,-1) in python from n-1 down to 1, so i from n-1 to 1 inclusive.

 so it's correct. 

 Code: 
Note: the array a is given as a[1] to a[n], so in 0-based, a[0] to a[n-1]

We need to read n and the array.

Final code:
```
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_sum = sum(a)
if total_sum % 3 != 0:
    print(0)
else:
    target = total_sum // 3
    # prefix[0] = 0, prefix[i] for i in 1 to n is sum of first i elements
    prefix = [0]
    for i in range(n):
        prefix.append(prefix[-1] + a[i])
    
    count = 0
    freq = {}
    # i from n-1 down to 1 (0-based)
    for i in range(n-1, 0, -1):
        if prefix[i+1] == target:
            count += freq.get(2 * target, 0)
        freq[prefix[i+1]] = freq.get(prefix[i+1], 0) + 1
    print(count)
```

Let me test with the first example: n=5, a=[1,2,3,0,3]
- total_sum=9, target=3
- prefix = [0,1,3,6,6,9]
- i from 4 down to 1:
- i=4: prefix[5]=9 !=3, count=0, freq{9:1}
- i=3: prefix[4]=6 !=3, count=0, freq{9:1,6:1}
- i=2: prefix[3]=6 !=3, count=0, freq{9:1,6:2}
- i=1: prefix[2]=3 ==3, count += freq.get(6,0) = 2, count=2, then freq{9:1,6:2,3:1}
- output 2 ✓

Example 2: [0,1,-1,0] → n=4, a=[0,1,-1,0]
- total_sum=0, target=0
- prefix = [0,0,1,0,0]
- i from 3 down to 1:
- i=3: prefix[4]=0 ==0, count += freq.get(0,0) =0, then freq{0:1}
- i=2: prefix[3]=0 ==0, count += freq.get(0,0) =1, count=1, then freq{0:2}
- i=1: prefix[2]=1 !=0, count unchanged
- output 1 ✓

Example 3: [4,1] n=2, sum=5, 5%3!=0, output 0 ✓

It works. 

Note: in the loop, when i=0, we don't enter, and i+1 in the condition is from 1 to n, and in prefix, we have indices 0 to n, so i+1 from 1 to n, which is valid.

So the code is ready. 

We must be careful with the indexing, and it matches the examples.

Final code:
```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_sum = sum(a)
if total_sum % 3 != 0:
    print(0)
else:
    target = total_sum // 3
    prefix = [0]
    for num in a:
        prefix.append(prefix[-1] + num)
    
    count = 0
    freq = {}
    # Iterate i from n-1 down to 1 (0-based)
    for i in range(n-1, 0, -1):
        if prefix[i+1] == target:
            count += freq.get(2 * target, 0)
        freq[prefix[i+1]] = freq.get(prefix[i+1], 0) + 1
    print(count)
```
